        White can play `d5xc5`. Now Black has lost a pawn.

        What about `a7a5`?

        White can play `d5xc5`.

        Since `c5c4` loses the pawn, the options are:

        2. Protect c5 with another piece.

        `b7b6` is also a strong move, and it adds a second defender to c5.

        White might play `a4-a5`.
        Then Black can play `e7f6`.
        White might move the king `d5-e5` or `d5-c6`.
        If `d5-c6`, Black can play `b4-d2` or similar.

        `b7b6` looks like the most solid move. It secures the c5 pawn and limits the White king's options.

    White King can reach f3 in two moves (e3, then f3).

    If we advance f4-f3, White King might play Ke3. Then the pawn on f3 is attacked.
    Black King at c6 cannot protect f3.
    Black King would need to be at e4, f4, g4 or e3, f3, g3 or e2, f2, g2.

    Wait, the g7 pawn is also there. It's far back.
    The priority is to coordinate the King and the advanced pawn.

    Let's try to bring the Black King closer to the f-pawn.
    Current: c6. Target: e5 or f5.
    Move c6d6? Or c6d5?
    c6d5 puts the King closer to f4 and blocks the White King from advancing to d4.
    If c6d5, White King at d3 has limited moves. He can't go to d4.

    Wait, if Black plays c6d5, and White plays Ke3...
    Then Black can play f4-f3.
    Now f3 is protected by the King at d5? No, d5 is not adjacent to f3.
    Adjacent to f3: e3, f3, g3, e2, f2, g2, e4, f4, g4.
    d5 is not one of these.

    Actually, let's look at the distance again.
    d5 and f3:
    dx = 6-4 = 2
    dy = 5-3 = 2

    What if Black plays f4f3 first?
    If f4f3, White King can play Ke3.
    Then Black King at c6 can move to d6.
    Still not protecting.

    Wait, if the goal is to promote, the King must accompany the pawn to protect it from the enemy King.
    Black King at c6, pawn at f4.
    The pawn needs to go f4 -> f3 -> f2 -> f1.
    The King should move towards the f-file and down.
    c6 -> d6 -> e6 -> f6 ... or c6 -> d5 -> e5 -> f5 ...

    If I play c6d5:
    1. It restricts the White King (cannot move to d4).
    2. It brings the King closer to the pawn.

    Let's check other moves. g7g6 or g7g5.
    Advancing the second pawn is good, but the f4 pawn is the immediate threat.

    Compare c6d5 with f4f3.
    If f4f3, White's most active response is Ke3.
    If c6d5, White's responses are Ke3, Kc3, etc.

    In the endgame of K+P vs K, the key is "opposition" and the "critical squares".
    For a pawn on f4, the critical squares are f5, e5, g5 (if the pawn is on f4, the critical squares are usually 2 ranks ahead, but it's a bit different since it's already on f4).
    Actually, if the pawn is on f4, and the Black King is on d5, they are controlling a lot of space.

    `c6d7,c6c7,c6b7,c6d6,c6b6,c6d5,c6c5,c6b5,g7g6,f4f3,g7g5`
